Optimization using response surface methodology (RSM)

This section examined the optimization process and evaluation of parameters for the profiles \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{f}{Re}_{x}^{0.5}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}{Re}_{x}^{-0.5}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}{Re}_{x}^{-0.5}$$\end{document} by the RSM. The wall drag \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{f}{Re}_{x}^{0.5}$$\end{document}, Nusselt number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}{Re}_{x}^{-0.5}$$\end{document}, and Sherwood number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}{Re}_{x}^{-0.5}$$\end{document} were simulated using a robust statistical method called RSM, taking into account a variety of variables, including nanoparticle concentration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varnothing}_{A{l}_{2}{O}_{3}}$$\end{document}, wedge angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta$$\end{document}, Brinkman parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document}, Brownian motion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${N}_{b}$$\end{document}, thermophoresis parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${N}_{t}$$\end{document}, and thermal radiation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Nr$$\end{document}. A brief description of the findings obtained for these thermophysical properties is given.

RSM

RSM is a robust statistical and mathematical method that is frequently used in science, engineering, and scientific studies to simulate and optimize the relationship between several input parameters and an outcome variable. RSM can be used in the setting of shear rate to investigate and comprehend how different elements affect a fluid’s or material’s shear rate, and eventually to determine the ideal circumstances that optimize or reduce the shear rate in accordance with particular goals55,56. For every dependent variable in the RSM technique, a model is developed that explained the primary and interaction impacts of various factors on each variable independently. According to57, the multivariate framework follows as:

30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{Z}={\mathcal{y}}_{0}+{\sum}_{i=1}^{3}{\mathcal{y}}_{i}{\mathcal{x}}_{i}+{\sum}_{i=1}^{3}{\mathcal{y}}_{ii}{\mathcal{x}}_{i}{\mathcal{x}}_{i}+{\sum}_{i=1}^{3}{\mathcal{y}}_{ij}{\mathcal{x}}_{i}{\mathcal{x}}_{j}$$\end{document}

Where, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{Y}$$\end{document} is the variable of interest, while the variables, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{y}}_{0}$$\end{document} is the intercept, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{y}}_{i}$$\end{document} is the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ith$$\end{document} factor of linear regression coefficient, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{y}}_{ii}$$\end{document} is its quadratic regression coefficient, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal{y}}_{ij}$$\end{document} is its interaction with the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$jth$$\end{document} factor. The objective of this approach is to create a proper link among independent variables and the response variable by optimizing the response of the variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{Z}$$\end{document}. Response surface approach typically requires 19 degrees of freedom and 20 runs pertaining to three layers of variables. The wedge angle parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta$$\end{document}, Brinkman number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document}, and volume friction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varnothing}_{A{l}_{2}{O}_{3}}$$\end{document} are chosen as the input variables in this problem. Table 5, Table 6 and Table 7 displays these factors and their levels for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{f}{Re}_{x}^{0.5}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}{Re}_{x}^{-0.5}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}{Re}_{x}^{-0.5}$$\end{document}, respectively. For each variable, three tiers are taken into account. These three levels are upper (+1), central (0), and minimal (−1). The radius of the curve is estimated in this study using Central Composite Face (CCF) centered design. The CCF are factorial structures with center points that are finished with a set of lateral points. A response parameter with curvature can be modeled using this technique. This can be accomplished by incorporating the axial and central points into a factorial design that has already been completed. In CCF, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${2}^{F}+2F+z$$\end{document} trials are required (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F=3$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z=6$$\end{document} represent the number of factors and central points, respectively58). Experimental design and response outputs of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Res \left({Sh}_{x}{Re}_{x}^{-0.5}\right)$$\end{document}.

Analysis of variance (ANOVA)

In response surface technique, the measurement of variance i.e., ANOVA is typically used to assess the level of model correctness. This statistical estimators used in this analysis are the adjusted mean squares (Adj.MS), degree of freedom (DoF), sum of squares (SS), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F-$$\end{document} value, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p-$$\end{document} value. Tables 11, 12, and 13 display these data. It is important to note that the data variation about the mean is measured by the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F-$$\end{document} value. More precision for the supplied data is indicated by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F-$$\end{document} value larger than unity. Tables 11, 12, and 13 show that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{f}{Re}_{x}^{0.5}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}{Re}_{x}^{-0.5}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}{Re}_{x}^{-0.5}$$\end{document} response, all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F-$$\end{document} value are higher than unity. Consequently, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F-$$\end{document} value is adequate to satisfy the criteria. Furthermore, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p-$$\end{document} values are calculated to assess the model from a statistical perspective. The models are confirmed for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p-$$\end{document} values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<$$\end{document} 0.05 based on the analysis of variance. All \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p-$$\end{document} values are less than 0.05 for models, as shown in Tables 11, 12, and 13.Table 11ANOVA results for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} As is clear that, the coefficients of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{B}}^{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{A}}*{\mathbb{B}}$$\end{document} are insignificant terms for the skin friction, while the terms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{E}}^{2},{\mathbb{F}}^{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{E}}.{\mathbb{F}}$$\end{document} of Nusselt number are insignificant terms. In a similar way, the coefficients of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{H}}^{2},{\mathbb{I}}^{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{G}}\mathbb{*}{\mathbb{H}}$$\end{document} are insignificant terms. The following equations can be used to express the significant relationship between each response and the independent variables:

For each response for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{f}{Re}_{x}^{0.5}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}{Re}_{x}^{-0.5}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}{Re}_{x}^{-0.5}$$\end{document}, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${R}^{2}$$\end{document} and adjusted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${R}^{2}$$\end{document} are computed in Tables 8, 9, and 10. It is important to note that if the values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${R}^{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${AdjR}^{2}$$\end{document} are close to unity. As a result, there is a strong statistical agreement between the independent variables and each response. This criterion is fulfilled for all three models. The skin friction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{f}{Re}_{x}^{0.5}$$\end{document} analysis reveals that the model is extremely significant, with a p-value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<$$\end{document} 0.0001 and an extremely large F-value. The most important element is the volume fraction of nanoparticles, which accounts for 87.86% of the variation in skin friction. This means that increasing the number of nanoparticles significantly raises drag on the wedge surface. The independent effects of the wedge angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta$$\end{document} and Brinkman parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document} are modest but still noteworthy. The model is similarly statistically valid for the heat transfer rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Nu_{x} Re_{x}^{ - 0.5}$$\end{document}. With approximately similar impacts 39–41%, the wedge angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta$$\end{document} and nanoparticle volume fraction are the most important variables. This indicates that improving heat transfer depends on both the wedge geometry and the concentration of nanoparticles. Although it has a smaller impact, the radiative parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Nr$$\end{document} is equally important. The mass transfer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Sh_{x} Re_{x}^{ - 0.5}$$\end{document} model is important. The most important factor, accounting for 67.90% of the variation, is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta$$\end{document}. This implies that, under the circumstances under study, the wedge-induced flow geometry has a greater impact on the concentration boundary layer than the Brownian motion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{b}$$\end{document} and thermophoresis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{t}$$\end{document} parameters.

Figs. 11, 12, and 13 display the residual plots for each response of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{f} Re_{x}^{0.5}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Nu_{x} Re_{x}^{ - 0.5}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Sh_{x} Re_{x}^{ - 0.5}$$\end{document} respectively. The normal probability for residual distribution are plotted in order to determine whether the findings are normal. It is evident that each response normal probability plot is a straight line. This shows that the regression model is sufficient and relevant, and it fits the observed data well58. These figures show the residuals in relation to the fitted values. The adjusted and observed outcomes have a strong connection, as this diagram illustrates. Sensitivity analysis (SA)

SA is a method for figuring out how the input parameters would influence a response. This type of study specifies the degree of response to variations in the supplied variables’ values. Scientists in their design procedure can therefore utilize it. According to Table 14, the sensitivity of parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{C}}$$\end{document} has the highest positive sensitivity, indicating that it is the primary contributor causing drag. The highest sensitivity −0.00122 of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{f} Re_{x}^{0.5}$$\end{document} is achieved, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{A}} = 1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{C}} = 1$$\end{document} keeping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{B}} = 0$$\end{document}. This is because the effective viscosity of the NF increases significantly as the particle loading increases. On the other hand, when, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{A}} = - 1$$\end{document}, consistently show negative sensitivity; for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{B}} = 0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{C}} = - 1$$\end{document}, which is −1.59571, suggesting that a higher wedge angle can lower the surface drag in these circumstances. Furthermore, at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{B}} = 0$$\end{document}, the Brinkman parameter has a favorable influence on reduced drag. The sensitivity of heat transfer rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Nu_{x} Re_{x}^{ - 0.5}$$\end{document} are quantified in Table 15. According to the results, the parameters with the highest positive sensitivity 3.16959 is attained at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{D}} = - 1$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{F}} = 0$$\end{document}. This indicates that the two most potent factors for improving heat transfer are the wedge geometry and the concentration of nanoparticles. Although parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{E}}$$\end{document} again exhibits a positive sensitivity, its impact is more erratic and contingent upon the concentrations of other variables. Table 16 quantifies the sensitivity of mass transfer rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Sh_{x} Re_{x}^{ - 0.5}$$\end{document}. A crucial result of the investigation is that parameter coded parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{I}}$$\end{document} (the thermophoresis parameter) consistently shows a negative sensitivity. A highest negative sensitivity −0.60402 is attained at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{G}} = - 1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{H}} = - 1$$\end{document}. This indicates that by pushing nanoparticles away from the wall, thermophoresis inhibits mass transfer at the wall. On the other hand, under the identical conditions, parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{G}} = - 1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{H}} = 1$$\end{document} exhibit positive sensitivities indicating that they are the primary causes for increasing the Sherwood number.

The response surface plots in Figs. 14, 15, and 16 provide contour plots, a visual depiction of the intricate relationships between parameters and their combined influence on the system’s outputs. The dramatically sloping surface in that corner of Fig. 14, which maps the combined effects on skin friction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{f} Re_{x}^{0.5}$$\end{document}, clearly illustrates that the strongest drag forces occur at the highest values of both the wedge angle and the nanoparticle volume fraction. This significant synergistic effect reveals that the geometric acceleration caused by the wedge and the enhanced viscosity from adding nanoparticles interact in conjunction to substantially amplify resistance at the surface. Likewise, Fig. 15 shows the Nusselt number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Nu_{x} Re_{x}^{ - 0.5}$$\end{document} shows a strong productive interaction, especially between the wedge angle and the nanoparticle volume fraction, where the surface tension peaks sharply. When both parameters are at their highest, confirming that the best way to maximize heat 13transfer rates is to simultaneously leverage the higher thermal conductivity of a denser NF and the flow acceleration from a steeper wedge. The Sherwood number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Sh_{x} Re_{x}^{ - 0.5}$$\end{document} response is shown in Fig. 16. The surface displays a noticeable curvature with the thermophoresis parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{t}$$\end{document} as well as a prominent incline with the wedge angle. This suggests that, although the flow geometry is the main source of mass transfer, the mechanism of thermophoresis (nanoparticle migration due to temperature gradients) also plays a significant, non-linear role in strengthening the concentration BL. When taken as a whole, these statistics shed light on the crucial parameter interactions that are necessary to achieve a truly optimal system, going beyond the individual factor effects displayed in The local rate of change, or sensitivity, for each engineering output skin friction, Nusselt number, and Sherwood number in response to changes in their corresponding controlling parameters is shown in detail in the sensitivity graphs (Figs. 17, 18, and 19). In order to clearly isolate the effects of each parameter, these graphs were created by keeping two parameters at set coded levels (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-$$\end{document} 1, 0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+$$\end{document} 1) and methodically altering the third. As an illustration, the skin friction graphs (Fig. 17) clearly show how sensitive it is to the nanoparticle volume fraction, where even slight changes result in a strong, nearly linear increase in drag, highlighting the dominant role seen in the ANOVA data. The sensitivity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{f} Re_{x}^{0.5}$$\end{document} for various fixed levels of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{A}}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{B}}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{C}}$$\end{document} are sketched in Fig. 17(a), Fig. 17(b) and Fig. 17(c). The positive slopes in each sub-figure confirm the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{f} Re_{x}^{0.5}$$\end{document} is most responsive to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{C}} = 1$$\end{document}. When the Brinkman parameter is at its lowest level (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{B}} = - 1$$\end{document}), the sensitivity is highest, suggesting that the drag-increasing effect of nanoparticles is significant, when frictional drag is low. Conclusions

This study examined a comprehensive sensitivity and computational analysis of heat and mass transfer in two-dimensional BLF of an experimental \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Al_{2} O_{3} - H_{2} O -$$\end{document} NF over an unsteady moving wedge embedded in a Darcy-Brinkman porous medium. A modified Buongiorno NF model and thermal radiation are incorporated into the mathematical model. The BVP4c method was used to solve the governing equations numerically after conversion into a system of ODEs. The refined Buongiorno NF model with experimental data-based efficient correlations has been applied to guarantee accurate modeling. A quadratic regression model for the rate of heat and mass transmission in terms of operational variables is presented and optimized using the RSM. This work main advancement is the use of RSM and ANOVA to statistically optimize and assess the sensitivity of important engineering parameters, such as skin friction, Nusselt number, and Sherwood number. The following constitute some of the study’s main findings:An increase in the Brinkman parameter, the fluid velocity increases because the viscous shear term takes precedence over the Darcy resistive term, resulting in a larger velocity profile.The velocity profile declines with porosity parameter. The enhanced permeability reduces the solid matrix impedance and the constraining drag force.The temperature of the NF increases with an increasing in the porosity parameter and the Brinkman parameter. Similar trend was observed for thermophoresis parameter and the thermal radiation parameter.The concentration profile showed declining trend when the thermophoresis parameter and the Brinkman parameter are increased. In a similar vein, a rise in the thermophoresis parameter pushes nanoparticles farther into the cooler mainstream fluid, which causes the concentration in the near-wall BL to significantly decrease.The linear terms in the RSM model explained 91.98% variation for the skin friction coefficient. While the two-factor interaction terms were mostly responsible for the leftover variation in the model, indicating that synergistic effects between the input parameters are important for drag optimization.The skin friction coefficient was found to be most quantitatively significantly influenced by the Brinkman parameter.All responses of skin friction, Nusselt number, and Sherwood number, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{2}$$\end{document} values surpassing 99.8%, showing that the created RSM models are extremely meaningful and accurate, demonstrating a significant statistical relationship between the input parameters and the system outputs.The critical parameter associations are revealed by response surface plots, which show that the highest drag and heat transfer rates occur concurrently at the greatest values of both the wedge angle and nanoparticle concentration. This is important for designing systems that need to balance energy consumption and performance.

An increase in the Brinkman parameter, the fluid velocity increases because the viscous shear term takes precedence over the Darcy resistive term, resulting in a larger velocity profile.

The velocity profile declines with porosity parameter. The enhanced permeability reduces the solid matrix impedance and the constraining drag force.

The temperature of the NF increases with an increasing in the porosity parameter and the Brinkman parameter. Similar trend was observed for thermophoresis parameter and the thermal radiation parameter.

The concentration profile showed declining trend when the thermophoresis parameter and the Brinkman parameter are increased. In a similar vein, a rise in the thermophoresis parameter pushes nanoparticles farther into the cooler mainstream fluid, which causes the concentration in the near-wall BL to significantly decrease.

In a similar vein, a rise in the thermophoresis parameter pushes nanoparticles farther into the cooler mainstream fluid, which causes the concentration in the near-wall BL to significantly decrease.

The linear terms in the RSM model explained 91.98% variation for the skin friction coefficient. While the two-factor interaction terms were mostly responsible for the leftover variation in the model, indicating that synergistic effects between the input parameters are important for drag optimization.

The linear terms in the RSM model explained 91.98% variation for the skin friction coefficient. While the two-factor interaction terms were mostly responsible for the leftover variation in the model, indicating that synergistic effects between the input parameters are important for drag optimization.

The skin friction coefficient was found to be most quantitatively significantly influenced by the Brinkman parameter.

The skin friction coefficient was found to be most quantitatively significantly influenced by the Brinkman parameter.

All responses of skin friction, Nusselt number, and Sherwood number, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{2}$$\end{document} values surpassing 99.8%, showing that the created RSM models are extremely meaningful and accurate, demonstrating a significant statistical relationship between the input parameters and the system outputs.

All responses of skin friction, Nusselt number, and Sherwood number, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{2}$$\end{document} values surpassing 99.8%, showing that the created RSM models are extremely meaningful and accurate, demonstrating a significant statistical relationship between the input parameters and the system outputs.

The critical parameter associations are revealed by response surface plots, which show that the highest drag and heat transfer rates occur concurrently at the greatest values of both the wedge angle and nanoparticle concentration. This is important for designing systems that need to balance energy consumption and performance.

The critical parameter associations are revealed by response surface plots, which show that the highest drag and heat transfer rates occur concurrently at the greatest values of both the wedge angle and nanoparticle concentration. This is important for designing systems that need to balance energy consumption and performance.

Limitation of the research

This study’s main limitation lies in computational model for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Al_{2} O_{3} - H_{2} O -$$\end{document} NF which depends on particular experimental relationships. This limits the findings applicability to other types of nanoparticles or higher volume fractions where intricate processes like particle arrangement, accumulation, and non-Newtonian behavior could call into question the hypothesis of a robust, homogeneous suspended particle. Additionally, the optimal thermal efficiency and statistical trends found through Response Surface Methodology may not accurately reflect the behavior of the system in very different manufacturing regimes or under extreme unsteady flow conditions because the sensitivity analysis and optimization results are intrinsically local to the established ranges of the Darcy-Brinkman and wedge parameters.

Future scope

Future investigations may expand the present model to three-dimensional and transient flow arrangements by adding further physical factors as fluctuating fluid characteristics, chemical processes, and magnetic fields. To further increase computing efficiency and forecast accuracy, hybrid data-driven approaches that combine physics informed neural networks (PINNs) with adaptive random sampling and measurement of uncertainty can be investigated.
